by constructing a sequence
Q^{\prime\prime}_{1},\dots,Q^{\prime\prime}_{n+1}
which is pointwise included in the former,
and such that
Q^{\prime\prime}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup Q^{\prime\prime}_{n+1}
is one exponential smaller than
Q^{\prime}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup Q^{\prime}_{n+1}
.
We define the following sets:

\displaystyle Q^{\prime\prime}_{n+1}=Q^{\prime}_{n+1},\qquad Q^{\prime\prime}_{n}=Q^{\prime}_{n},

\displaystyle Q^{\prime\prime}_{n-1}=Q^{\prime\prime}_{n}\cup\{(g,P_{k+1},\dots,P_{m})\in Q^{\prime}_{n-1}\ |\ \forall i.|P_{i}|\geq 1,\ \sum_{i}|P_{i}\setminus\{p^{n}\}|\leq 1\},

\displaystyle Q^{\prime\prime}_{i}=Q^{\prime\prime}_{i+1}\cup\bigcup_{g\in\mathcal{R}_{i}}\{g\}\times\left(2^{Q^{\prime\prime}_{i+1}}\right)^{m-k}\qquad\mbox{for $1\leq i\leq n-2$},

where
g=(p,a(u_{1},\dots,u_{k},b(v_{1},\dots,v_{m^{\prime}}),u_{k+1},\dots,u_{m})\rightarrow S,r)
and
\mathsf{ord}(b)=i
.
Thus to
Q^{\prime\prime}_{n-1}
we only add those states
(g,P_{k+1},\dots,P_{m})
where all
P_{i}
except one are equal to
\{p^{n}\}
, and the remaining one is either a singleton or a pair
\{q,p^{n}\}
for some state
q
.
We can see that all transitions in
\Delta^{\prime}
will only use states from
Q_{1}^{\prime\prime}
.
To prove this, recall that
\Delta^{\prime}
is the least set containing
\Delta\cup\Delta_{0}
and closed under applying the saturation procedure.
The initial set
\Delta\cup\Delta_{0}
only uses states from
Q^{\prime}_{n+1}\subseteq Q_{1}^{\prime\prime}
.
Thus, suppose that the current set of transitions uses only states from
Q_{1}^{\prime\prime}
;
we should prove that transitions added by (one step of) the saturation procedure also use only states from
Q_{1}^{\prime\prime}
.
Notice that all states in the considered run tree
t
on
r
appear in some transition, so they come from
Q_{1}^{\prime\prime}
. The only “new” state is
(g,P_{k+1},\dots,P_{m})
created for a deep rule.
Let
b
be the root of the special subtree of the left side of
g
.
If
\mathsf{ord}(b)\neq n-1
, then we have
(g,P_{k+1},\dots,P_{m})\in Q^{\prime\prime}_{\mathsf{ord}(b)}\subseteq Q^{\prime\prime}_{1}
,
since, as already observed,
P_{k+1},\dots,P_{m}\subseteq Q^{\prime\prime}_{1}\cap Q^{\prime}_{\mathsf{ord}(b)+1}=Q^{\prime\prime}_{\mathsf{ord}(b)+1}
.
For
\mathsf{ord}(b)=n-1
, we recall that each of the sets
P_{k+1},\dots,P_{m}
describes a subtree of order
n
,
so it is either of the form
\{p^{n}\}
or
\bigcup t(r^{-1}(\mathsf{x}))
for some variable
\mathsf{x}
of order
n
.
But, as observed previously, at most one of these sets may contain a state from
Q^{\prime}_{+}
.
However (as required in an non-
n
-alternating automaton) all states of order
n
except
p^{n}
are from
Q^{\prime}_{+}
.
Thus
\sum_{j=k+1}^{m}|P_{j}\setminus\{p^{n}\}|\leq 1
,
and in consequence
(g,P_{k+1},\dots,P_{m})\in Q_{n-1}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq Q_{1}^{\prime\prime}
.
This finishes the proof that only states from
Q_{1}^{\prime\prime}
are used.

Notice that
|Q^{\prime\prime}_{n-1}|\leq|Q^{\prime}_{n}|+|\calR|\cdot 2(m-1)\cdot|Q_{n}^{\prime}|
(while
|Q^{\prime}_{n-1}|
is exponential in
|Q_{n}^{\prime}|
),
where
m
is the maximal rank of any symbol in
\Sigma
.
Consequently,

|Q^{\prime\prime}_{1}|\leq\mathsf{exp}_{n-2}(O((|Q|+|\mathcal{R}|)\cdot m\cdot|\mathcal{R}|))
and
|\Delta^{\prime}|\leq\mathsf{exp}_{n-1}(O((|Q|+|\mathcal{R}|)\cdot m\cdot|\mathcal{R}|))
.

are the same as in the main text.

Figure 1:
Translation from annotated higher-order pushdown systems to AOTPSs

Appendix D
The translation for Krivine machines

We present graphically the rewrite rules of the resulting ordered
tree transition system. The rules in
Figure
2
are the same as in the main text. We
hope that the graphical presentation better conveys the
intuition behind them.

(const
\mbox{}_{2}
)

Figure 2:
Translation from the Krivine machine to AOTPSs

Appendix E
Encoding AOTPS into the Krivine machine with states

See
8

The encoding is performed in four steps.

Step 1: Flattening the AOTPS

In this step we show that the l.h.s. of rewrite rules of AOTPSs can be flattened,
in the sense that the only lookahead that the system has is in deep rules,
and all other subtrees are just variables.
We recall that a rule
p,l\to S,r
is
flat
if
l
is either of the form
a(\mathsf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{m})
(shallow rule) or
a(\mathsf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{k-1},b(\mathsf{y}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{y}_{m^{\prime}}),\mathsf{x}_{k+1},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{m})
(deep rule);
an AOTPS is
flat
when all its rules are flat.

Theorem 19
.

Every AOTPS
\mathcal{S}
can be converted into an equivalent flat AOTPS
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
of exponential size.

Fix an AOTPS
\calS=\langle n,\Sigma,P,\calR\rangle
.
We create an equivalent flat AOTPS
\calS^{\prime}=\langle n,\Gamma,P,\calR^{\prime}\rangle
as follows.
Let
\mathsf{sub}(\calR)
contain all proper (that is, other than the whole tree) subtrees of
l
for all rules
p,l\to S,r
in
\calR
.
The intuition is that the new system will store in each node with
k
children a tuple consisting of
k
subsets of
\mathsf{sub}(\calR)
; the
i
-th of them will contain these patterns that match to the tree at the
i
-th child.
In this way, when a rule
p,l\to S,r
is applied,
it is sufficient to read in the root whether appropriate subpatterns of
l
match to subtrees starting in children of the root, instead of testing a longer part of the tree.
Thus, as the new alphabet
\Gamma
we take
\bigcup_{a\in\Sigma}\{a\}\times(2^{\mathsf{sub}(\calR)})^{\mathsf{rank}(a)}
,
where the rank and the order of a symbol in
\Gamma
is inherited from its
\Sigma
coordinate.
For a
\Sigma
-tree
t
we obtain the
\Gamma
-tree
\mathsf{enc}({t})
by labelling each node
u
with the tuple of
\mathsf{rank}(t(u))
sets where the
i
-th set contains those trees
l^{\prime}\in\mathsf{sub}(\calR)

that match the subtree rooted at the
i
-th child of
u
(that is, for which this subtree equals
l^{\prime}\sigma
for some substitution
\sigma
).

Consider some rule
p,l\to S,r
.
Thanks to the additional labeling of
\mathsf{enc}({t})
,
seeing only the label in the root of
t
we know whether
l
matches to
t
.
This allows us to replace every
r
-ground subtree of
l
by an
r
-ground variable, and obtain
l
in one of the two forms allowed in a flat rule.
On the other hand, we have to ensure that
r
creates a tree with the correct labelling.
This is possible since our labelling is compositional:
the tuple in a node can be computed basing on labels of its children.

More precisely, for each rule
p,l\to S,r
we create new rules as follows.
Let
l=a(u_{1},\dots,u_{m})
, and, if the rule is deep, let
u_{k}=b(v_{1},\dots,v_{m^{\prime}})
be the lookahead subtree of
l
.
For all sets
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}\subseteq\mathsf{sub}(\calR)
such that
u_{1}\in X_{1},\dots,u_{m}\in X_{m}
, and (in the case of a deep rule) for all sets
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{m^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathsf{sub}(\calR)
such that
v_{1}\in Y_{1},\dots,v_{m^{\prime}}\in Y_{m^{\prime}}
there will be one new rule.
8
8

8

Some sets in
\mathsf{sub}(\calR)
are “inconsistent”, as well as some sets
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{m^{\prime}}
may be “inconsistent” with
X_{k}
.
New rules using such sets are redundant, but it also does not hurt to add them, as anyway they cannot be applied to any tree of the form
\mathsf{enc}({t})
.

If
p,l\to S,r
is shallow, as the new left side we take
(a,X_{1},\dots,X_{m})(\mathsf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{m})
, where we leave
\mathsf{x}_{i}=u_{i}
if
u_{i}
is a variable, and we take a fresh variable
\mathsf{x}_{i}\not\in\calV(r)
of order
\mathsf{ord}(u_{i})
otherwise (when
u_{i}
is
r
-ground).
If
p,l\to S,r
is deep, as the new left side we take
(a,X_{1},\dots,X_{m})(\mathsf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{k-1},(b,Y_{1},\dots,Y_{m^{\prime}})(\mathsf{y}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{y}_{m^{\prime}}),\mathsf{x}_{k+1},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{m})
,
where again we leave subtrees being variables and we replace other subtrees by fresh variables.
The choice of
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
and
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{m^{\prime}}
assigns sets of “matching patterns” to all variables:

\displaystyle\mathsf{mp}(\mathsf{x}_{i})=X_{i},

\displaystyle\mathsf{mp}(\mathsf{y}_{i})=Y_{i}.

Then in a bottom-up manner we can assign such sets to all subtrees of
r
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{mp}(c(w_{1},\dots,w_{j}))=\{\mathsf{z}\in\mathsf{sub}(\calR)\mid\mathsf{ord}(\mathsf{z})=\mathsf{ord}(c)\}\cup

\displaystyle\qquad\cup\{c(w_{1}^{\prime},\dots,w_{j}^{\prime})\in\mathsf{sub}(\calR)\mid w_{1}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{mp}(w_{1}),\dots,w_{j}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{mp}(w_{k})\}.

The new right side of the rule is
\llbracket r\rrbracket
with
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
defined by:

\displaystyle\llbracket w\rrbracket=(c,\mathsf{mp}(w_{1}),\dots,\mathsf{mp}(w_{j}))(\llbracket w_{1}\rrbracket,\dots,\llbracket w_{j}\rrbracket)

\displaystyle\mbox{if }w=c(w_{1},\dots,w_{j}),

\displaystyle\llbracket w\rrbracket=w

\displaystyle\mbox{if $w$ is a variable}.

We remark that in order to recover correct marking of the right side of a rule it was necessary to mark a node by patterns matching to children of that node instead of patterns matching to the node itself (the latter marking would be insufficient).
It is easy to see that each transition of
\langle\calC_{\calS},\to_{\calS}\rangle

can be faithfully simulated by a transition of
\langle\calC_{\calS^{\prime}},\to_{\calS^{\prime}}\rangle
.

Step 2: Eliminating control locations

To ease the presentation in step 3, we now remove control locations from the AOTPS.
To allow alternation, we have to extend slightly the definition of an AOTPS.
The rules will be now of the form
l\rightarrow R
, where
R
is a set of trees
r
such that
l\rightarrow r
is a rewrite rule.
The resulting alternating transition system
\langle\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{S}},\rightarrow_{\mathcal{S}}\rangle
has
\Sigma
-trees as configurations,
and, for every configuration
t
, and set of configurations
U
there is a transition
t\rightarrow_{\mathcal{S}}U
if
there exists a rule
l\rightarrow R
of
\mathcal{S}
and a substitution
\sigma
s.t.
t=l\sigma
and
U=\{r\sigma\ |\ r\in R\}
.

Control locations can be encoded in the root symbol of the pushdown tree.
The new alphabet is
\Sigma^{\prime}=\Sigma\cup(\Sigma\times P)
,
where new symbols in
\Sigma\times P
inherit order and rank from the
\Sigma
-component.
Let
p,l\rightarrow S,r
be a shallow rule of the original system,
with
l=a(\mathsf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{m})
and
r=c(s_{1},\dots,s_{k})
(the case for a deep rule is analogous).
Then, the new system has a rule
l^{\prime}\rightarrow R
(with no control locations),
with
l^{\prime}=(a,p)(u_{1},\dots,u_{m})
and
R=\{(c,q)(s_{1},\dots,s_{k})\ |\ q\in S\}
.
There is a problem when
r
is just a variable
\mathsf{x}
(necessarily occurring in
l
).
In this case, we use a deep rule by guessing the root symbol
c
at the (unique) position of
\mathsf{x}
in
l
(below
a
).
That is, for every shallow rule
p,l\rightarrow S,\mathsf{x}_{k}
of the original system,
and for every symbol
c
of rank
h
,
we introduce a deep rule
l^{\prime}\rightarrow R
,
where
l^{\prime}=(a,p)(\mathsf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{k-1},c(\mathsf{y}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{y}_{h}),\mathsf{x}_{k+1},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{m})

and
R=\{(c,q)(\mathsf{y}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{y}_{h})\ |\ q\in S\}
.
We can assume that in the original system there are no deep rules of the form
p,l\rightarrow S,\mathsf{x}
,
as those can be broken down into two rules, a deep one where the r.h.s. is not a variable,
and a shallow one where the r.h.s. is a variable.
Thus, we do not have consider any other case when removing control locations.

Notice that while starting from a flat AOTPS, the obtained AOTPS without control locations is also flat.

Step 3: From AOTPSs to higher-order recursion schemes

After the first two steps we have a flat AOTPS without control locations (where rules are of the form
l\rightarrow R
with
R
a set of trees).
Our goal is to translate an AOTPS of this form into a Krivine machine,
thus proving Theorem
8
.
In order to obtain a more natural translation, we use recursion schemes, a model quite similar to the Krivine machine.

We first define alternating higher-order recursion schemes with states.
We use types as defined in Sec.
4.3
.
However, instead of
\lambda Y
-terms, we use applicative terms, where moreover we can use typed nonterminals from some set
\calN
.
An
applicative term
is either (i) a constant
a^{0^{\mathsf{rank}(a)}\to 0}\in\Gamma
, (ii) a variable
x^{\alpha}\in\calV
, (iii) a nonterminal
A^{\alpha}\in\calN
, (iv) an application
(M^{\alpha\to\beta}\,N^{\alpha})^{\beta}
.
An
alternating recursion scheme with states
of level
n\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
is a tuple
\calG=\langle n,\Gamma,Q,\calN,\calR,\Delta\rangle
, where
\langle\Gamma,Q,\Delta\rangle
is an alternating tree automaton,

\calN
is a finite set of nonterminals of level at most
n
,
and
\calR
is a function assigning to each nonterminal
A
in
\calN
of type
\alpha_{1}\to\dots\to\alpha_{k}\to 0
a rule of the form

A\,x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\,\dots\,x_{k}^{\alpha_{k}}\to M
, where
M
is an applicative term of type
0
with free variables in
\{x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}},\dots,x_{k}^{\alpha_{k}}\}
, constants from
\Gamma
, and nonterminals from
\calN
.
A recursion scheme
\calG
induces an alternating transition system
\langle\calC_{\calG},\to_{\calG}\rangle
,
where in a configuration
(p,M)\in\calC_{\calG}
we have
p\in Q
and
M
is a closed applicative term of type
0
using constants from
\Gamma
and nonterminals from
\calN
.
We have two kinds of transitions.
First, for each rule
A\,x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\,\dots\,x_{k}^{\alpha_{k}}\to M
in
\calR
we have a transition

(p,A\,M_{1}\,\dots\,M_{k})\to_{\calG}\{(p,M[M_{1}/x_{1},\dots,M_{k}/x_{k}])\}
.
Second, for every
(p\xrightarrow{a}P_{1}\dots P_{k})\in\Delta
we have a transition
(p,a\,M_{1}\,\dots\,M_{k})\to_{\calG}(P_{1}\times\{M_{1}\})\cup\dots\cup(P_{k}\times\{M_{k}\})
.

Fix a flat AOTPS
\calS=\langle n,\Sigma,\calR\rangle
without control locations.
An
extended letter
is a pair
(a,o)
where
a\in\Sigma
and
o\colon\{1,\dots,\mathsf{rank}(a)\}\to\{1,\dots,n\}
.
The meaning is that the letter is
a
and its children have orders
o(1),\dots,o(\mathsf{rank}(a))
.
For each extended letter
(a,o)
we will have a corresponding nonterminal
A_{a,o}
.
A first approximation of the encoding is that a tree
a(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})
will be represented as
A_{a,o}
to which encodings of
u_{1},\dots,u_{k}
are applied as arguments.
Then the rule for the nonterminal
A_{a,o}
can simulate all shallow rules of our system
\calS
, constructing any term having
u_{1},\dots,u_{k}
as subterms.
Notice that rules of
\calS
are flat, so we need not to look inside
u_{1},\dots,u_{k}
; however we need to know their orders—that is why we assign nonterminals to extended letters not just to letters.

Nevertheless, there are also deep rules.
In order to handle them, each tree
a(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})
will be represented in multiple ways.
One representation will handle shallow rules.
Moreover, for each extended letter
(b,o^{\prime})
and
{\hat{\imath}}
we will have a nonterminal
A_{a,o}^{b,o^{\prime},{\hat{\imath}}}
,
and
a(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})
will be represented as
A_{a,o}^{b,o^{\prime},{\hat{\imath}}}
with applied representations of
u_{1},\dots,u_{k}
.
This nonterminal will be still waiting for subtrees of a potential parent of our
a
, having label
b
; when they will be applied, the nonterminal will simulate deep rules of
\calS
having
b(\dots,a(\dots),\dots)
on the left side,
where the
a
is on the
{\hat{\imath}}
-th position.
As we have multiple encodings of a tree, we need to keep them in parallel.
Thus
A_{a,o}
(and similarly
A_{a,o}^{b,o^{\prime},{\hat{\imath}}}
) instead of taking one argument for each subtree,
takes multiple arguments for each subtree, one for each encoding of the subtree.

Let
\overline{\Sigma}_{k}
be the set of all triples
(b,o^{\prime},{\hat{\imath}})
, where
(b,o^{\prime})
is an extended letter for
b\in\Sigma
, and
o^{\prime}({\hat{\imath}})=k
.
Let us fix some (arbitrary) total order on elements of
\overline{\Sigma}_{k}
, that will be used to order arguments of our terms.
Using the product notation
\alpha\times\beta\to\gamma
for the type
\alpha\to\beta\to\gamma
, we define types

\displaystyle\Psi_{k}=0\times\prod_{(a,o,{\hat{\imath}})\in\overline{\Sigma}_{k}}\alpha_{a,o,>k},

where the
(a,o,{\hat{\imath}})
are ordered according to our fixed order on
\overline{\Sigma}_{k}
.
We have used here the types

\displaystyle\alpha_{a,o,>k}=\Psi_{o(i_{1})}\to\dots\to\Psi_{o(i_{m})}\to 0,

where
i_{1}<\dots<i_{m}
is the list of all
i\in\{1,\dots,\mathsf{rank}(a)\}
for which
o(i)>k
.
In particular we have
\alpha_{a,o,>n}=0
.
Notice that
\alpha_{a,o,>k}
and types in
\Psi_{k}
are of level at most
n-k
.
We are now ready to define the type of our nonterminals:
A_{a,o}
has type

\displaystyle\Psi_{o(1)}\to\dots\to\Psi_{o(\mathsf{rank}(a))}\to 0

and
A_{a,o}^{b,o^{\prime},{\hat{\imath}}}
has type

\displaystyle\Psi_{o(1)}\to\dots\to\Psi_{o(\mathsf{rank}(a))}\to\alpha_{b,o^{\prime},>\mathsf{ord}(a)}.

Recall that the resulting higher-order recursion scheme has to use constants and an alternating tree automaton to simulate alternation.
We will have two kinds of constants in
\Gamma
:
\vee_{i}
of type
0^{i}\to 0
, and
\wedge_{i}
of type
0^{i}\to 0
, defined for appropriate numbers
i\in\mathbb{N}
(we need only finitely many of them).
The constant
\vee_{i}
simulates nondeterministic choice, and
\wedge_{i}
simulates universal choice.
The tree automaton
\langle\Gamma,Q,\Delta\rangle
is defined in the natural way:
Q
consists of a single state
q
,
and we have transitions

\displaystyle q\xrightarrow{\vee_{i}}\emptyset\dots\emptyset Q\emptyset\dots\emptyset,\qquad q\xrightarrow{\wedge_{i}}Q\dots Q.

In particular there is no transition reading
\vee_{0}
, and we have the transition
q\xrightarrow{\wedge_{0}}\varepsilon
.

Next, we define our encoding of trees into applicative terms.
In the definition below we use tuples just as a shorthand:
M\,(N_{1},\dots,N_{k})
is intended to mean
M\,N_{1}\,\dots\,N_{k}
.
A tree is encoded as a tuple of applicative terms:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Enc}(u)=(\mathsf{enc}(u),\mathsf{enc}^{b_{1},o_{1},{\hat{\imath}}_{1}}(u),\dots,\mathsf{enc}^{b_{k},o_{k},{\hat{\imath}}_{k}}(u))

where
(b_{1},o_{1},{\hat{\imath}}_{1})<\dots<(b_{k},o_{k},{\hat{\imath}}_{k})
are all the elements of
\overline{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{ord}(u)}

ordered according to the fixed order on
\overline{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{ord}(u)}
.
We have here one basic encoding:

\displaystyle\mathsf{enc}(a(u_{1},\dots,u_{m}))=A_{a,o}\,\mathsf{Enc}(u_{1})\,\dots\,\mathsf{Enc}(u_{k})\qquad\mbox{where }\forall i\cdot o(i)=\mathsf{ord}(u_{i}).

Additionally for each
(b_{j},o_{j},{\hat{\imath}}_{j})\in\overline{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{ord}(a)}
we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{enc}^{b_{j},o_{j},{\hat{\imath}}_{j}}(a(u_{1},\dots,u_{m}))=A^{b_{j},o_{j},{\hat{\imath}}_{j}}_{a,o}\,\mathsf{Enc}(u_{1})\,\dots\,\mathsf{Enc}(u_{k})\qquad\mbox{where }\forall i\cdot o(i)=\mathsf{ord}(u_{i}).

It remains to define rules for the nonterminals from the rules of
\calS
.
Let us use the convention on variable naming that every left side of a rule in
\calR
is of the form
a(\mathsf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{m})
or
b(\mathsf{y}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{y}_{{\hat{\imath}}-1},a(\mathsf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{m}),\mathsf{y}_{{\hat{\imath}}+1},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{k})
.
Recall that
\calS
is flat, so every left side is of such form. A right side
R=\{r_{1},\dots,r_{l}\}
of a rule is encoded as

\displaystyle\mathsf{enc}(R)=\wedge_{l}\,\mathsf{enc}(r_{1})\,\dots\,\mathsf{enc}(r_{l}),

where
\mathsf{enc}(r_{i})
is defined as for normal trees, with the encoding of variables given by:

\displaystyle\mathsf{enc}(\mathsf{x}_{i})=x_{i},

\displaystyle\mathsf{enc}^{a,o,{\hat{\imath}}}(\mathsf{x}_{i})=x_{i}^{a,o,{\hat{\imath}}},

\displaystyle\mathsf{enc}(\mathsf{y}_{i})=y_{i},

\displaystyle\mathsf{enc}^{a,o,{\hat{\imath}}}(\mathsf{y}_{i})=y_{i}^{a,o,{\hat{\imath}}}.

Here,
\mathsf{x}_{i}
is a variable of the AOTPS, while
x_{i}
is a variable in an applicative term of the recursion scheme,
and similarly for the other variables.
In order to define a rule for a nonterminal
A_{a,o}
we look at all rules with
a(\mathsf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{m})
on the left side with
\mathsf{ord}(\mathsf{x}_{i})=o(i)
for all
i
.
Let
R_{1},\dots,R_{l}
be the right sides of these rules.
Then we take

\displaystyle A_{a,o}\,\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{x}_{1})\,\dots\,\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{x}_{m})\to\vee_{l+m}\,\mathsf{enc}(R_{1})\,\dots\,\mathsf{enc}(R_{l})\,\mathsf{deep}_{1}\,\dots\,\mathsf{deep}_{m}

with

\displaystyle\mathsf{deep}_{{\hat{\imath}}}=x_{{\hat{\imath}}}^{a,o,{\hat{\imath}}}\,\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{x}_{i_{1}})\,\dots\,\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{x}_{i_{d}}),

where
i_{1}<\dots<i_{d}
are all the
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
for which
o(i)>o({\hat{\imath}})
.
In order to define a rule for a nonterminal
A^{b,o^{\prime},{\hat{\imath}}}_{a,o}
we look at all rules having on the left side
b(\mathsf{y}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{y}_{{\hat{\imath}}-1},a(\mathsf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{m}),\mathsf{y}_{{\hat{\imath}}+1},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{k})

with
\mathsf{ord}(\mathsf{y}_{i})=o^{\prime}(i)
and
\mathsf{ord}(\mathsf{x}_{i})=o(i)
for all
i
, and
\mathsf{ord}(a)=o^{\prime}({\hat{\imath}})

(in particular for nonterminals
A^{b,o^{\prime},{\hat{\imath}}}_{a,o}
with
\mathsf{ord}(a)\neq o^{\prime}({\hat{\imath}})
there are no such rules).
Let
R_{1},\dots,R_{l}
be the right sides of these rules (when
\mathsf{ord}(a)\neq o^{\prime}({\hat{\imath}})
, we always have
l=0
).
Then we take

\displaystyle A_{a,o}^{b,o^{\prime},{\hat{\imath}}}\,\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{x}_{1})\,\dots\,\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{x}_{m})\,\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{y}_{i_{1}})\,\dots\,\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{y}_{i_{d}})\to\vee_{l}\,\mathsf{enc}(R_{1})\,\dots\,\mathsf{enc}(R_{l}),

where
i_{1}<\dots<i_{d}
are all the
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
for which
o^{\prime}(i)>\mathsf{ord}(a)
.

Let us briefly see the correspondence between steps of
\calS
and steps of
\calG
.
Consider a tree
u=a(u_{1},\dots,u_{m})
.
Its encoding
\mathsf{enc}(u)
is
A_{a,o}\,\mathsf{Enc}(u_{1})\,\dots\,\mathsf{Enc}(u_{m})
where
o(i)=\mathsf{ord}(u_{i})
for each
i
.
The first step of
\calG
performed from
\mathsf{enc}(u)
results in the right side of the rule for
A_{a,o}
where we substitute
\mathsf{enc}(u_{i})
for
x_{i}
and
\mathsf{enc}^{b,o^{\prime},{\hat{\imath}}}(u_{i})
for
x_{i}^{b,o^{\prime},{\hat{\imath}}}
for each
i,b,o^{\prime},{\hat{\imath}}

(denote this substitution as
[\varphi]
).
The resulting tree starts with some
\vee_{j}
, so then
\calG
nondeterministically chooses one of its subtrees.
There is a subtree
\mathsf{enc}(R)[\varphi]
for each shallow rule
a(\mathsf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{m})\to R
of
\calS
where
\mathsf{ord}(\mathsf{x}_{i})=o(i)=\mathsf{ord}(u_{i})
for each
i
.
These are exactly all shallow rules that can be applied to
u
.
By applying this rule to
u
we obtain the set
\{r\sigma\mid r\in R\}
where
\sigma
maps
\mathsf{x}_{i}
to
u_{i}
for each
i
.
Notice that
\mathsf{enc}(R)[\varphi]
starts with
\wedge_{|R|}
and has as children
\mathsf{enc}(r)[\varphi]
for each
r\in R
.
Thus
\calG
in the next step will transit into the set
\{\mathsf{enc}(r)[\varphi]\mid r\in R\}
.
Finally, we see that
\mathsf{enc}(r\sigma)=\mathsf{enc}(r)[\varphi]
.

Another possibility for
\calG
in the second step is to transit for some
{\hat{\imath}}\in\{1,\dots,m\}
to

\displaystyle\mathsf{deep}_{{\hat{\imath}}}[\varphi]

\displaystyle=(x_{{\hat{\imath}}}^{a,o,{\hat{\imath}}}\,\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{x}_{i_{1}})\,\dots\,\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{x}_{i_{d}}))[\varphi]=\mathsf{enc}^{a,o,{\hat{\imath}}}(u_{{\hat{\imath}}})\,\mathsf{Enc}(u_{i_{1}})\,\dots\,\mathsf{Enc}(u_{i_{d}})=

\displaystyle=A^{a,o,{\hat{\imath}}}_{b,o^{\prime}}\,\,\mathsf{Enc}(v_{1})\,\dots\,\mathsf{Enc}(v_{k})\,\mathsf{Enc}(u_{i_{1}})\,\dots\,\mathsf{Enc}(u_{i_{d}}),

where
u_{{\hat{\imath}}}=b(v_{1},\dots,v_{k})
, and
o^{\prime}(i)=\mathsf{ord}(v_{i})
for each
i
, and
i_{1}<\dots<i_{d}
are all
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
for which
o(i)>o({\hat{\imath}})
. The next three steps of
\calG
simulate exactly all deep rules of
\calS
having on the left side
a(\mathsf{y}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{y}_{{\hat{\imath}}-1},b(\mathsf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{k}),\mathsf{y}_{{\hat{\imath}}+1},\dots,\mathsf{y}_{m})

with
\mathsf{ord}(\mathsf{y}_{i})=o(i)
and
\mathsf{ord}(\mathsf{x}_{i})=o^{\prime}(i)
for all
i

(in the same way as it was for shallow rules and for the nonterminal
A_{a,o}
).
It is important that the right sides of such rules use only those variables
\mathsf{y}_{i}
for which
\mathsf{ord}(\mathsf{y}_{i})=o(i)>o({\hat{\imath}})=\mathsf{ord}(b)
.
We conclude that
\langle\calC_{\calS},\to_{\calS}\rangle
and
\langle\calC_{\calG},\to_{\calG}\rangle
faithfully simulate each other.

Step 4: From higher-order recursion schemes to the Krivine machine

It is well-known how to translate from one formalism to the other; cf.
[
25
]
.

Appendix F
Ordered annotated multi-pushdown systems

We encode ordered annotated multi-pushdown systems
[
15
]
into AOTPSs.
Formally, an
alternating ordered annotated multi-pushdown system
is a tuple
\mathcal{R}=\langle m,n,\Gamma,Q,\Delta\rangle
,
where
m\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
is the number of higher-order pushdowns,

n\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
is the order of each of the
m
higher-order pushdowns,

\Gamma
is a finite pushdown alphabet containing a distinguished initial symbol
e
,

Q
is a finite set of control locations,
and
\Delta\subseteq Q\times\{1,\dots,m\}\times\Gamma\times O_{n}\times 2^{Q}

is a set of rules, where

O_{n}=\bigcup_{k=1}^{n}\{\mathsf{push}_{k}^{b},\mathsf{push}_{k},\mathsf{pop}_{k},\mathsf{collapse}_{k}\ |\ b\in\Gamma\}
.
Intuitively, a rule
(p,l,a,o,P)
can be applied when the control location is
p
and the topmost symbol on the
l
-th stack is
a
, and it applies the stack operation specified by
o
to this stack.
Pop and collapse operations are called
consuming
.
An alternating ordered annotated multi-pushdown system
\mathcal{R}

induces an alternating transition system
\langle\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{R}},\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}\rangle
, where

\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{R}}=Q\times\Gamma_{n}^{m}
,
and
(p,w_{1},\dots,w_{m})\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}P\times\{(w_{1}^{\prime},\dots,w_{m}^{\prime})\}

if, and only if, there exists a rule
(p,l,a,o,P)\in\Delta
s.t.

1)

w_{l}=\langle{a^{u_{l}},\cdots}\rangle
,

2)

if
o
is consuming, then
w_{1}^{\prime}=\dots=w_{l-1}^{\prime}=\langle{e^{\langle{\,}\rangle},\langle{\,}\rangle,\dots,\langle{\,}\rangle}\rangle
,

3)

if
o
is not consuming, then
w_{1}^{\prime}=w_{1},\dots,w_{l-1}^{\prime}=w_{l-1}
,

4)

w_{l}^{\prime}=o(w_{l})
, and

5)

w_{l+1}^{\prime}=w_{l+1},\dots,w_{m}^{\prime}=w_{m}
.

For
c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{R}}
and
T\subseteq Q
,
the
(control-state) reachability problem
for
\mathcal{R}
asks whether

c\in\mathrm{Pre}^{*}({T\times\Gamma_{n}^{m}})
.

Encoding.

Let
\Sigma
be an ordered alphabet containing,
for every pushdown index
l\in\{1,\dots,m\}
and order
k\in\{1,\dots,n\}
,

1)

an end-of-stack symbol
(l,k,\bot)
of order
(l-1)\cdot n+k
and rank
0
,

2)

a symbol
(l,k)
of order
(l-1)\cdot n+k
and rank
k+2
,

3)

a symbol
(l,\bullet)
of order
(l-1)\cdot n+1
and rank
n+2
.

Moreover,
\Sigma
contains a symbol
\bullet
of order
1
and rank
m\cdot(n+1)
, and,
for every pushdown index
l\in\{1,\dots,m\}
and every
a\in\Gamma
, a symbol
(l,a)
of order
(l-1)\cdot n+1
and rank
0
.
Thus
\Sigma
has order
mn
.
Notice that the size of
\Sigma
is
\left|{\Sigma}\right|=O(m\cdot(n+\left|{\Gamma}\right|))
.
Fix a pushdown index
l
.
An empty order-
k
pushdown is encoded as the tree
\mathsf{enc}_{l,k}({\langle{\,}\rangle})=(l,k,\bot)
.
A nonempty order-
k
pushdown
\langle{a^{\hat{u}},u_{1},\dots,u_{k}}\rangle
is encoded as the tree

\displaystyle\mathsf{enc}_{l,k}({\langle{a^{\hat{u}},u_{1},\dots,u_{k}}\rangle})

\displaystyle=\hbox to222.5pt{\vbox to42.45pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 105.86517pt\lower-34.12128pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

and

\displaystyle\mathsf{enc}^{\bullet}_{l}({\langle{b^{\hat{u}},u_{1},\dots,u_{n}}\rangle})

To simplify the encoding, we assume w.l.o.g. that collapse links are always of order
n
(collapse to a lower order is still allowed though).
An
m
-tuple of nonempty order-
n
pushdowns

w=(\langle{a_{1}^{\hat{u}_{1}},u_{1,1},\dots,u_{1,n}}\rangle,\dots,\langle{a_{m}^{\hat{u}_{m}},u_{m,1},\dots,u_{m,n}}\rangle)

is encoded as the following tree

where
t^{\bullet}_{l}=\mathsf{enc}^{\bullet}_{l}({\hat{u}_{l}})
for every
l=1,\dots,m
,
and
t_{l,k}=\mathsf{enc}_{l,k}({u_{l,k}})
for every
l=1,\dots,m
and
k=1,\dots,n
.
We can observe here two differences when comparing to the encoding of annotated higher-order pushdown systems.
First, we do not put the topmost stack symbol in the root, but in an additional child of the form
(l,a)
just below the root.
This allows to avoid an alphabet of exponential size containing all
m
-tuples of letters from
\Gamma

(the rest of the stack is encoded in an analogous way, but this is only for uniformity).
Second, at the beginning of each collapse link we use a special letter
(l,\bullet)
instead of
(l,k)
.
This letter has a fixed order, and thanks to that we can use a variable of this fixed order to match the subtree encoding a collapse link.
Recall that for annotated higher-order pushdown systems we have created separate rules for each possible order of the collapse links,
but here such a solution would result in an exponential blowup since we have
m
collapse links of independent orders.

Let
\langle m,n,\Gamma,Q,\Delta\rangle
be an ordered annotated multi-pushdown system.
We define an equivalent AOTPS
\mathcal{S}=\langle mn,\Sigma,Q,\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}}\rangle

of order
mn
,
where
\Sigma
and
Q
are as defined above,
and
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}}
contains a rule for each rule in
\Delta
.
We use the convention that variables
\mathsf{x}_{l,k}
,
\mathsf{z}_{l,k}
have order
(l-1)\cdot n+k
, and
variables
\mathsf{y}_{l}
,
\mathsf{y}^{\prime}_{l}
,
\mathsf{t}_{l}
, are of order
(l-1)\cdot n+1
.
We write
\mathsf{x}_{l}^{i..j}
(with
i\leq j
) for the tuple of variables
(\mathsf{x}_{l,i},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{l,j})
.
If
(p,l,a,\mathsf{push}_{k}^{b},P)\in\Delta
, then there is the following shallow rule in
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}}
:

This deep rule satisfies the ordering condition since
(l,k)
has order
(l-1)\cdot n+k
,
and all variables
\mathsf{x}_{l}^{k+1..n}
that appear also on the r.h.s. have order strictly higher than
(l,k)
.
Notice that
y_{l}^{\prime}
has relatively low order, but it does not appear on the r.h.s.
Finally, if
(p,l,a,\mathsf{collapse}_{k},P)\in\Delta
, then there is the following deep rule in
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}}
:

The deep rule above satisfies the ordering condition since
(l,\bullet)
has order
(l-1)\cdot n+1
,
and all variables
\mathsf{x}_{l}^{k+1..n}
,
\mathsf{t}_{l+1}
,
\mathsf{y}_{l+1}
,
\mathsf{x}_{l+1}^{1..n}
,
\dots
,
\mathsf{t}_{m}
,
\mathsf{y}_{m}
,
\mathsf{x}_{m}^{1..n}
that appear also on the r.h.s.
have strictly higher order.

Lemma 20

(Simulation)
.

We have that
(p,w)\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}P\times\{w^{\prime}\}
if, and only if,

(p,\mathsf{enc}({w}))\rightarrow_{\mathcal{S}}^{*}P\times\{\mathsf{enc}({w^{\prime}})\}
.
Thus, the (control-state) reachability problem for
\mathcal{R}
is equivalent to the reachability problem for
\mathcal{S}
.
